- MEMORANDUM FOR THE RECORD

SUBJECT: James A. Noel

. 1. - Chief, DO and I talked to Jim Noel this morning about
the possibility.of his converting from staffer to carcer agent.
After considerable discussion, Jim agreed to the conversion. He
is obviously doing this reluctantly, but he appreciates the gituation
within the DDP in which the encumbrance of senior grades by longe
time employees is blocking the promotions of junior officers.

2. Jim urged that he be retained aa a career agent until 7 .
he reaches the age of mandatory retirement (about three years).
Chief, DO stated that the need in the San Francisco area for handlers
«of contacts and assets is such that there would be a continuing need
for experienced career agents on the outside of the small San
Francisco Base. I told Jim that unless he pulled something stupid,
he was virtually assured of career agent status until he reached
age 60, and perhaps could be employed as a retired annuitant after
that time if his performance was productive and if operational
requirements remain as they are at present,

3. Jim explained why he could; not retire -~ the next five -
yeare will be difficult for him financially with two teen-age boys to

put through college.

4, Chief, DO and Jira will work out the details and timing
of his conversion to career agent. ;

. Dove iia Li

SUBJECT t Ret treasat “Information

1. There are a few situations in which en employee at the cine of
retirement may have so many years of service (almost 37) that he would —

and “transferred to the Civil Service Systema. Since you will complete 37

and also 1f returned to the Civil Service System, The following infor~
mation 1a being submitted to assist you in determining which course of

ection you desire to follow:

a. During the period from 15 August 1944 through 19 October
1946 there were no retirement deductions withheld from your salary. . A
In order for thie time co be credited in couniing total service co
under the Organization System it would be necessary for you to make
a deposit, with interest, to cover this perfod. The waximua annuity
under our system is 70 per cent. based on 35 yeara of creditable
service, If you should return to the Civil Service System in order |
to receive the maximum annuity it would be necessary for you to make
a doposit, with interest, to covec that period fur which no contri-
butions were made; however, you may receive fuli credit in counting
total service without making a deposit but your annuity will be
reduced by one-tenth of the amount due as deposit.

b. As you are aware, you are presently entitled to apply for
voluntary retirement under the Orgenization Retirement and Disabil-~
ity System. The annuity estiaste given below as of 31 October 1967 H
would be applicable if a deposii were wade to cover the period for
which no deductions were withheld, The annuity estimate ylven for. !
31 March 1968 would be applicable withouc a deposit since you would
have completed 35 years of creditable service at that time without \

Memorandum in Lieu of Fitness Report for Cecil J. KLOBUKAR |
for the Period 31 March 1969 to 31 March 1970 :

Cecil J. KLOBUKAR, a career agent, has been primarily targeted
against the local consular community during this reporting per=
fod, For this purpose he has maintained membership in two

local organizations concerned with foreign affairs, More than
that, ho has been an active contributor to their programming

and has participated in most of their social and. cultural
functions. This has enabled him to come in contact with a rela-
tively large number of consulate officers. During the past year
he has met, on one or more occasions, thirty consular members.
He is concentrating, however, on the ten consulates which have
representation in the denied and semi-denied areas. Within this
area he in further rotining the target by attempting to contact
the more junior officers who give promise of a longer future

. Gareer and a greater chanco of being posted to an area of opera-

tional interest. To date, he has succeeded in establishing such
contacts in the Japanese, Finnish and Indian Consulates. In this
last case he has successfully passed tho target to another Base
officer for further development and hopes to accomplish the same
with his Japanese and Finnish contacts.

In addition to this primary responsibility, KLOBUKAR also handles
a support asset who is targeted against the academic community.
He is directing this asset's efforts in the cultivation of two
promising leads -- one, Japanese and the other, Vietnamese,

Both of these cases are progressing very well due in great part
to the direction and guidance which KLOBUKAR gives to the support

asset.
KLOBUKARK also played a very important and direct role in the
assessment and intermediate cultivation of TWPARROT/1 and

He

TWPARROT/2 who were subsequently successfully recruited.
is now developing a local QKWAFER student who is an interesting,

but difficult, recruitment prospect.

‘ KLOBUKAR 4s cost conscious in the expenditure of operational

funds; he does not. have supervisory responsibilities.

_27 May 1969

Memorandum in. Licu of Fitness. Report for Cecil J. KLOBUKAR

for the Period 26 July 1968 to 31 March 1969

,
a

Cecil J. KLOBUKAR was the Chief of this Base until
July 1968 at which tisie he was assigned new duties as a
career agent, targeted primarily against the San Francisco
consular community. Until that time thoro was no specific
program aimed at the official foreign community. It was t
KLOBUKAR's responsibility, therofore, to break ground in \
this new target aréa. He went about this chore itn an :
orderly and organized manner by refining the community to. \
those which wero of priority interest because of their rep- t
resetitation in denied and semi-denied areas, He then started :
looking for a means to get information and gain access to
members of this target group. To assist him in this endeavor
he recruited two members of the consular community, repre~
senting Latin American countries. Since their performance
did not live up to expectations, KLOBUKAR took the tnitiative
by joining three local organizations relating toe foreign
affairs in order to enhance his cover and possibility of
direct access as a retired LNGOLD officer. By participating
in the gatherings of these organizations, KLOBUKAR is. aiming
at direct contact with at least a few of the officers from

target consulates,

KLOBUKAR has demonstrated excellent initiative in pur-
suing.as many avenues of approach as possible to this rather
difficult target. It is not a cohesive community and does
not experience the usual mixing and contact that one finds
in normal diplomatic circles. It is fragmented and cliquish
and for this reason KLOBUKAR has had to show both imagination
and perseverance in probing for access to those specific
consulates in which the Base has priority interest. He is
now demonstrating his determination to get at this target
by exploring another possible means ot approach of his own

device,

In addition to his primary responsibility against the
consular target, KLOBUKAR also handles one support asset
currently being used against a high priority student target
and threo general purpose assets who are used from time to

time in both the business and academic areas. Here he has ~
done excellent work, particularly in the direction and guid-

ance. of the first asset.

KLOBUKAR is cost-conscious ‘in ‘the expenditure of opera-
tional funds; he does not presently have any supervisory

a -responsibility.

As has been indicated above, KLOBUKAR has been given a
very difficult primary target on which the Base had very. .
little information or prior experience. While it has been
a frustrating and sometimes exasperating assignment,
KLOBUKAR has shown both initiative and perseverance in
attacking it; his performance has been Strong.

1. Subject, who was born 19 March 1911, 18 a GS-16 and
has been assigned to Madrid as Chief of Station since
October 1961.

2. As Chief of Station, he is charged with the organ-
ization and management of the station and is rosponsible
for the planning, direction, and supervision of all its
activities. He also exercises general supervision of the
Barcelona base. Assisting him and under his general auper-
vision are 24 steffi employoss and 15 contract omployees.

3. Subject serves as the Ambassador's principal intel-
ligence officer, as chairman of the Rabassy Defector Committee,
as the Agency representative on the Embassy Country Toam,
and as the coordinator of U.S. clandestine intelligence opera
tions in Spain. Accredited ag the Agency '8 representative to
the Spanish Intelligence (AEM) ond Becurity (DGS) services,
he plans, directs, and supervises all aspects of tho station's
multi-level liaison with these services and personally handles
the liaison with the Service chicfa and A number of their
senior officers. On the operational side, subject handles
BISHAD/1, CITIZEN ond QUWIND/1. He recruited QUIP/1 during
the rating period, arranged n new cover job for HARRIOTT
through QUWIND/1, and participated in the handling of QUMALT

and QUHOPS.

4. Subject is an expertenced and mature officer,
possessing an excellent command of Spanish. Highly motivated,
conscientious, and industrious, he rendily accepts responsi-
bility and is both thorough and reliable in the execution of
bis duties. Even tempered and affable, he holds the confi-
dence and friendship of thos

whom he is responsibic, pnd the moraie of
noticeably high. He is alert to the opera

of any given situation, evidences scund and objective judgment,

. responsibility to others is meaningful and well delineated.

. 5. “ Subject's performance has been strong throughout the
rating period. He hag given the station purposeful direction
and ‘competent management and as an effective supervisor who
commands the loyalty and reapect of those whom he supervises, he
has succeeded in obtaining a maximum effort from the station
personnel. _He enjoys the confidence of the Ambassador and other
principal officers, of the Embassy, several of whom have expressed
thetr appreciation of subject's work and commented on the value
of the station's contribution to the functioning of the Embassy.
The fact that the Ambassador arrived in Madrid with definite
prejudices regarding the Agency but was of quite another mind
upon bis departure is testimony to subject's skill and tact.

6. Subject has conducted the Agency's liaison with the
Spanish services most competently, as evidenced by the rela-
tionship ha establiehed with Captain General Munoz Grandes, hia
ability to persuade the AEM to undertake sensitive operations
in Cuba, and the fact that this liaison did not suffer following
the attack on the Spanish ship "Sierra Aranzazu". Marked
progress has been achieved in joint CE and CP operations, and
gubject has developed QUANTUN/30 to the point wherein the
latter's collaboration far exceeds the cooperation normally
expected in an official liaison relationship.

_ 7% In addition to his considerable executive and liaison
duties, subject has carried out the previously cited opera-
tional assignments with marked ability. He is a convincing and
persuasive agent handler, and his operational reporting is

prompt and thorough,

8. The only criticism I would make of subject is that I
believe tho station should bemking a greater effort to develop
internal assets who will be able to report on and influence
developments in Spain following the departure of Franco.

SUBJECT : Mr. James A. Noel : :
Fitness Report Covering the Pertod-1h August 1961 to fl
t 31 March 1962 !

1. Mr. Noel assumed his duties as Chief of Station. Madrid on
: 23 September 1Y61. ‘The Station, aa constituted when Mr. Noel arrived,
inciuded somy 21: staffers plus numerous contract personnel, as weil as
@ two imn base in Burcelona. The Madrid Station proper not only includes
the Enbaouy Installation but an outside operations section as well. Since .
Mr. Noel arrived the Station bas been launched into a Cuban operations . .
program of considerable scale and on a cravh basis. In addition, in this
period, one State cover slot was withdrawn, andthe. experienced Chief of

Base, Barceloua had to be brought home due to a family emergency.

2. To date Mr. Noel has given every evidence of a particular ability
to cope with the crash program and the acute shortage of personnel with
dudiciousness and maturity. While it is too early, yet, to pass judgement,
there are (ndicationn that Mr. Noel has been preoccupied with Cutan
problens (an area of special interest to him) to the detriment of lower
priority but, nonetheleus, necessary Spanish targels (for example, contact
with the local opposition and a budding effort ayainot the Spanish Cormunisot

Party).

a 3. As indicated above, Nr. Hoel has not been in place as Chief of
‘ Station long enough tu justify firm reservations concerning his managenent, :
nor to permit meaningful criticism. Alco, as indicated above, his out-
. standing noticeable strength to date has been hia ability to cone witha
™ heavy operational prouram, a shortace of personnel, and 4 sometines difficult
_ operational climte, with judgenent, calaness and patience.

vUMMARY OF EMPLOYM1i.T

James A. Noel.

From 1931 to 19441 served aa a Foreign Service Officer in
various posts in Mexico. In 1944I joined OSS/SSU/CIG and from
that date until 1947, I worked in Europe and Latin America. In
1947 I again joined the Department of State, and served in many
posts in Latin America until 1966 when I returned to the United
States for a two (2) year detail with the Department of Army. In
1968 I retired from the Department of State and since then I have
been living in the San Francisco area doing occasional work ona
consultant basis for the Department of Defense. ’
